# Data files associated with A Dependency Map Enhanced with Next-Generation 3D Cancer Models


## Overview ##
This file set includes metadata files (6 files) and analysis result files (29 files) associated with Neiswender, Maffa, Brenan et al. "A Dependency Map Enhanced with Next-Generation 3D Cancer Models" (Nature, 2026). The custom analysis code used for generating these results is available at a GitHub repository (https://github.com/broadinstitute/DepMap-NextGen-Public).  


In addition, raw profiling data of the models (including data for mutation, copy number, gene expression, and gene dependency) and processed data files required for running the custom code are available at DepMap portal (https://depmap.org/portal/data_page/?tab=allData; file set name "NextGen Model Manuscript 2026").


## Metadata Files ##


### minipool_guide_metadata.csv
This table provides the barcode sequence (“Construct_Barcode”) and target gene (“Gene”) for each of the 2000 sgRNAs included in the minipool library. See Fig. 5c and Extended Data Fig. 9a for the schematic and graphical representations of this library, respectively. This library targets 1,000 genes, including 250 test genes, 650 negative control genes, and 100 positive control (i.e., common essential) genes.


### minipool_library_composition.csv
This table shows the membership of each of the nine core functional classes (“Integrin”, “ECM Adhesion”, “Actin Regulation”, “Adherens Junction”, “Tight Junction”, “Lipid Metabolism”, ”Wnt Signaling”, “PI3K mTOR Signaling”, and “Cell Cycle”) targeted by the minipool library. The majority of the test genes of this library belong to one or more of these core functional classes, while the rest of them (“Other”) do not belong to any of these classes but are still included in the library based on specific interests. See Extended Data Fig. 9a for the graphical representations of this library.


### minipool_screen_metadata.csv
This table provides the metadata, including model ID (“DepMap ID”), growth format, and type of culture medium (“Serum status” and “Culture medium”), as well as the QC results for each of the minipool screens (n = 18). These QC results include log2-fold change correlation between the replicates (“replicate LFC correlation”), total sequencing reads (“Total reads”), and two metrics based on the gene effect distributions of negative and positive controls (“NNMD” and “ROC-AUC”).


### minipool_sequence_metadata.csv
This table provides the metadata, including model ID (“DepMap ID”), growth format, and type of culture medium (“Serum status” and “Culture medium”), as well as the QC results for each of the minipool screen sequencing attempts (n = 34). Note that 16 out of the 18 minipool screens were conducted with technical duplicates. One of the replicates (0178_Adh_Nat_Serum_Dome_RepB) was removed from the further analysis due to the low NNMD score. The read count of the PCR products of genomic DNA prepared from Cas12a-null 293T cells that were infected with the minipool library (HEK_CasNull_MP_control) is also displayed.


### model_metadata.csv
This table provides the metadata, including model ID, lineage (“OncotreeLineage”), primary disease (“OncotreePrimaryDisease”), subtype (“OncotreeSubtype”), growth pattern (Adherent, Mixed, Suspension or Unknown for traditional and Dome or Spheroid for NextGen models), as well as the availability of WGS/RNAseq/CRISPR profiling results for the traditional (n = 1685) and NextGen (n = 314) models used in the study. For the models profiled by the CRISPR screen, the screen type (“CRISPRScreenType”), classified into five types based on the model type and culture condition—namely, 2DS (adherent/suspension culture of the traditional cell line models), 2DO (coat culture of the NextGen organoid models), 3DO (dome culture of the NextGen organoid models), 2DN (coat culture of the NextGen CNS models), and 3DN (spheroid culture of the NextGen CNS models)—is also indicated. Note that "GrowthPattern" indicates the format of culture used for the derivation of the respective model, which can be different from the format of culture used for screening and omics profiling (the latter is indicated by "CRISPRScreenType"; also see "Culture Format" column of Supplementary Table 1).


### screen_metadata.csv
This table provides the metadata, including the model ID, library, screen type, model lineage (“OncotreeLineage”), model primary disease (“OncotreePrimaryDisease”), model subtype(“OncotreeSubtype”), and growth pattern, for all the CRISPR screens of traditional (screen n = 1387; PassesQC = “TRUE”) and NextGen (n = 147) models whose results are analyzed in the study. The availability of gene expression (“HasExprData”) and CN data in the corresponding model is also indicated. The screen type was classified into five types based on the model type and culture condition, namely, 2DS (adherent/suspension culture of the traditional cell line models), 2DO (coat culture of the NextGen organoid models), 3DO (dome culture of the NextGen organoid models), 2DN (coat culture of the NextGen CNS models), and 3DN (spheroid culture of the NextGen CNS models). Note that "GrowthPattern" indicates the format of culture used for the derivation of the respective model, which can be different from the format of culture used for screening and omics profiling (the latter is indicated by "ScreenType"; also see "Culture Format" column of Supplementary Table 1).



## Analytical Result Files ##


### biomarker_expression_tumor_vs_normal.csv
For the three representative biomarker-associated dependencies shown in Fig. 2 and Extended Data Fig. 5 (HNRNPH1 and PROX1 expression addiction and ENO1/ENO2 paralog dependency), the expression of the biomarker (HNRNPH1, PROX1 and ENO2, respectively) was compared between the tumor samples and lineage-matched normal samples of the “TCGA TARGET GTEx” cohort downloaded from the Xena browser (https://xenabrowser.net; n = 3192 [normal], 4143 [tumor]; only samples from nine tissue types, biliary tract, colorectal, breast, CNS/brain, esophagus/stomach, ovary, pancreas, prostate and uterus, were analyzed). The sample number (“normalN” and “tumorN”) and mean expression score (“Mean_normal” and “Mean_tumor”) for the normal and tumor groups, as well as the absolute difference in mean expression (“absDelta”), are indicated. Statistical significance of the difference in expression between the two groups was scored using two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test (“MWU_p”). Adjusted P-values were calculated on the Mann-Whitney U test P-values using the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure (“MWU_q”).


### celligner_geneset_scores.csv
Expression scores for each of the 41 gene expression meta-programs identified by Gavish et al. (Nature, 2023 [PMID: 37258682]) as well as Basal and Classical programs defined by Moffitt et al. (Nat Genet, 2015 [PMID: 26343385]) were calculated from the gene expression data of the DepMap models (traditional and NextGen; n = 1914) and TCGA-plus tumor samples (n = 12236). See “Scoring the activity of gene-expression MPs” subsection of Methods for the strategy used for the calculation. The scores shown here underlie all the plots involving the meta-program expression scores, including those shown in Figs. 3a,b, 4a-e,g and Extended Data Figs. 6c,d, 7b-j, 8a,b.  


### celligner_nearest_neighbor_table.csv
For each of the DepMap models (n = 1909) and Novartis PDX models (n = 445; Gao et al. Nat Med, 2015 [PMID: 26479923]) (“input”), the identities (sample ID [“target”] and lineage [“target_lineage”]) of the 100 (25 for PDX models) nearest tumor samples (of the TCGA-plus/Met500 cohort [n = 13104]) on the Celligner UMAP embedding were recorded. This file was used for the Celligner-based model lineage prediction (Fig. 1f and Extended Data Figs. 2b,c).


### celligner_prediction_accuracy.csv
For each of the solid tumor models of DepMap NextGen (n = 304), DepMap traditional (n = 1346), and Novartis PDX (n = 443), their lineages were inferred from the lineages of the 25 (or more; indicated in the “neighbors_considered” column) nearest tumor samples on the Celligner embedding. DepMap models of the Ampulla of Vater and Vulva/Vagina lineages were removed from the further analysis due to the scarcity of tumor samples with corresponding lineages (n = 4 and 0, respectively; “immediate_fail”). The scores shown here underlie the Celligner lineage prediction accuracy plots shown in Extended Data Fig. 2a.


### celligner_undiff_cluster.csv
For each of the DepMap traditional (n = 1367) and NextGen models (n = 382), Novartis PDX models (n = 443), and TCGA-plus/Met500 tumor samples (n = 11029) of solid tumors, their residence in the undifferentiated cluster on the Celligner embedding is indicated (“undiff_cluster”). Note that some models have multiple gene expression profiles and the residence in the undifferentiated cluster was recorded per individual profiles. The undifferentiated cluster was defined on the basis of the relative abundance of the DepMap models and TCGA-plus/Met500 tumor samples as well as the mean  HALLMARK_EPITHELIAL_MESENCHYMAL_TRANSITION ssGSEA enrichment score among the DepMap models in the individual clusters identified on Celligner (cluster n = 79). Four clusters (clusters #28, 59, 62 and 67) were collectively designated as “undifferentiated cluster”. See “Lineage prediction with Celligner” subsection of Methods for the details of this procedure. The scores shown here underlie the plots for the proportion of samples in the undifferentiated cluster shown in  Extended Data Fig. 2c.


### expression_addiction_tests_full.csv
For each of the genes scored for both dependency and expression in the NextGen models (gene n = 17889), the Pearson correlation (r) between the dependency and the expression of the dependency gene itself (“NextGenPearsonR”) and the significance of correlation (by two-tailed Pearson correlation; “NextGenPValue”) were calculated. The corresponding Pearson correlation in the traditional models (“TraditionalPearsonR”) was also scored. In addition, the dependency class of the gene based on the dependency profiles across the DepMap NextGen models is indicated (“NextGenDepClass”; see “Classification of dependency profiles” subsection of Methods). Note that only the genes in the three strong dependency classes (“NextGenDepClass” strongly selective, high variance and pan-essential; gene n = 3786) were plotted in Fig. 2a.


### integrin_fak_colocalization_test.csv
To assess the colocalization between integrin subunits (integrin beta1, integrin alphaV) and FAK in the KP3 cells grown in either plastic (2D) or dome (3D) cultures, the images of integrin staining and FAK staining from the same region were loaded onto Fiji (ImageJ ver 1.54p) and the regions of interest (ROIs) were manually defined by drawing boundaries around single cells. Three metrics for protein colocalization—intensity correlation quotient (“Li’s ICQ”; Li et al. J Neurosci, 2004 [PMID: 15102922]), thresholded Manders' Coefficient 1 (“Mander’s tM1”), and Pearson correlation (“PearsonR”)—between the two stainings within the ROI was calculated using the Coloc2 plugin (ver 3.1.0) for Fiji. Data from four sample types ("Class"), integrin alphaV-staining, plastic (AV2D), integrin alphaV-staining, dome (AV3D), integrin beta1-staining, plastic (B12D), and integrin beta1-staining, dome (B13D), are shown Li’s ICQ values are plotted on Extended Data Fig. 10b.


### major_onc_tsg_3d_vs_2d_gene_full.csv
For the oncogenes and tumor suppressor genes (TSGs) that were frequently (> 1%) mutated in the TCGA cohort (n = 40 [oncogene], 50 [TSG]; CDKN2B was removed since this gene was not evaluated in the NextGen model CRISPR screen), their dependency was compared between the 3D organoids cultured as dome (n = 94) and lineage-matched traditional 2D adherent models (n = 433). Statistical significance of the difference in dependency was scored using two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test (“MWU_p”). Adjusted P-values were calculated on the Mann-Whitney U test P-values using the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure (“MWU_q”). For the cancer genes whose mutation (hotspot mutation for the oncogenes and damaging mutation for the TSGs) is annotated, the same analysis was conducted after removing the mutant models to see if the differing dependencies between the 2D models and 3D organoids holds even when we focus our analysis to the models with WT alleles of the respective gene (“mutfree_q” and “mf_confirmation”; also see “major_onc_tsg_3d_vs_2d_gene_full.csv”). The scores shown here underlie the plot shown in Extended Data Fig. 8d.


### major_onc_tsg_3d_vs_2d_mutfree_full.csv
For the oncogenes and tumor suppressor genes (TSGs) that were frequently (> 1%) mutated in the TCGA cohort (n = 40 [oncogene], 50 [TSG]), their dependency was compared between the 3D organoids cultured as dome and lineage-matched traditional 2D adherent models after removing the mutant models of the respective gene (see “major_onc_tsg_alteration_matrix.csv”). The numbers of models used in the comparison (i.e., non-mutant models) are shown in the “2D_N” and “3D_N” columns. Statistical significance of the difference in dependency was scored using two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test (“MWU_p”). Adjusted P-values were calculated on the Mann-Whitney U test P-values using the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure (“MWU_q”). 


### major_onc_tsg_3d_vs_2d_pathway_full.csv
For the oncogenes and tumor suppressor genes (TSGs) that were frequently (> 1%) mutated in the TCGA cohort (n = 40 [oncogene], 50 [TSG]), we classified them into one of the 10 oncogenic signaling pathways based on the function of the protein they encode. For the groups of oncogenes and the groups of TSGs that belong to the same pathway (13 groups), mean dependency scores for the genes within the same group were calculated for the individual models. Subsequently, the mean dependency scores (i.e., mean of the means) of the NextGen 3D organoids (n = 94) and lineage-matched traditional 2D models (n = 433) were compared. Statistical significance of the difference in mean dependency scores was assessed using two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test (“MWU_p”). Adjusted P-values were calculated on the Mann-Whitney U test P-values using the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure (“MWU_q”). The scores shown here underlie the plot shown in Extended Data Fig. 8f.


### major_onc_tsg_alteration_matrix.csv
For each of the oncogenes and tumor suppressor genes (TSGs) that were frequently (> 1%) mutated in the TCGA cohort (n = 40 [oncogene], 50 [TSG]), mutation (hotspot mutation for oncogenes and damaging mutations for TSGs) of the respective gene per individual models in the NextGen 3D organoids (n = 94) and lineage-matched traditional 2D cell lines (n = 433) were scored. The values indicate the allele frequency of the mutations: 0 = no mutation; 1, 0 < (allele frequency) ≤ 0.95; 2, (allele frequency) > 0.95. This matrix was used to remove mutant models in “major_onc_tsg_3d_vs_2d_mutfree_full.csv”.


### minipool_chronos_gene_effects.csv
Chronos gene dependency scores were determined based on the results of the minipool screens (screen n = 18) for each of the 1,000 genes evaluated in these screens (100 positive control genes, 650 negative control genes, and 250 test genes). These Chronos scores provided the basis for the minipool screen analyses whose results are presented in Figs. 5d-g, Extended Data Figs. 9c-g.


### minipool_growth_format_culture_medium_per_class.csv
After calculating the effects of growth formats and culture media on gene dependencies as coefficients of the linear regression model (see “Minipool screen: analysis” subsection of Methods; the coefficient values are shown in “minipool_regression_coefficients. csv”), two analyses were conducted to evaluate the gene class-specific effects of these two components (i.e., growth format and culture medium) of culture conditions: 1) Mann-Whitney U test to compare the distributions of coefficients between in-class and out-of-class test genes for each of the nine core functional classes of minipool targets (results shown in Extended Data Figs. 9f,g); 2) F-test to compare the variances of the coefficients between in-class and out-of-class test genes for each of the nine core functional classes of minipool targets (results shown in Fig. 5g). This table summarizes the results of these two analyses, where columns starting with “GF” indicate the effects of growth formats and those starting with “CM” represent the effects of culture media. The scores shown here underlie the plot shown in Fig. 5g and Extended Data Figs. 9f,g.


### minipool_growth_format_tests.csv
For each of the test genes assessed by the minipool screen (gene n = 250), the effect of the growth formats (Matrigel dome culture [dome] vs monolayer culture on plastic plates [plastic]) on gene dependency was extracted as a coefficient of the linear regression model (see “Minipool screen: analysis” subsection of Methods; these coefficients are shown in “minipool_regression_coefficients. csv”) and the significance of the effect was scored by the two-sided linear regression t-test (“pvalue”). FDR (adjusted P-value) was calculated using the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure. The mean Chronos scores in the screens on dome cultures (n = 11) and plastic cultures (n = 7) are also shown (“true_dome_mean” and “true_plastic_mean”, respectively). The scores shown here underlie the plots shown in Fig. 5d and Extended Data Fig. 9f.


### minipool_regression_coefficients.csv
For each of the genes assessed by the minipool screen (gene n = 1000), the effects of the growth formats (Matrigel dome culture [dome] vs monolayer culture on plastic plates [plastic) and the culture media (serum-free OPAC medium vs serum-containing RPMI/FBS medium) on gene dependency were extracted as coefficients of the linear regression model (see “Minipool screen: analysis” subsection of Methods). This spreadsheet shows the coefficients representing the effect of growth formats (“C(Growth_Format)[T.Plastic]”) and the effect of culture media (“C(Serum_status)[T.Serum-free]”) for each gene. The scores shown here underlie the plots shown in Figs 5d-g and Extended Data Figs. 9f,g.


### minipool_regression_pvalues.csv
In extracting the effects of growth formats and culture media on gene dependencies (gene n = 1000) via the linear regression model, the significance of these effects—against the null hypothesis that these components of culture conditions do not have any effect on gene dependency—were also scored using the two-sided linear regression t-test. This spreadsheet shows the P-values for the effect of growth formats (“C(Growth_Format)[T.Plastic]”) and the effect of culture media (“C(Serum_status)[T.Serum-free]”) per each gene. The scores shown here underlie the plots shown in Figs 5d,e.


### minipool_serum_status_tests.csv
For each of the test genes assessed by the minipool screen (gene n = 250), the effect of the culture media (serum-free OPAC medium vs serum-containing RPMI/FBS medium) on gene dependency was extracted as a coefficient of the linear regression model (see “Minipool screen: analysis” subsection of Methods; these coefficients are shown in “minipool_regression_coefficients. csv”) and the significance of the effect was scored by the two-sided linear regression t-test (“pvalue”). FDR (adjusted P-value) was calculated using the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure. The mean Chronos scores in the screens with serum-containing RPMI/FBS (n = 9) and serum-free OPAC (n = 9) are also shown (“true_serum_mean” and “true_serum_free_mean”, respectively). The scores shown here underlie the plots shown in Fig. 5e and Extended Data Fig. 9g.


### next_gen_cancer_gene_annotations.csv
For all the oncogenes and tumor suppressor genes (TSGs; both annotated by the OncoKB; https://www.oncokb.org), the number of the NextGen models (with CRISPR profiles [n = 147]) that have relevant alterations—hotspot mutation and/or amplification for oncogenes and damaging mutation and/or deletion for TSGs—were scored. Oncogenes and TSGs with sufficient number of altered NextGen models (n ≥ 5 and at least one model with a hotspot mutation) were subjected to the analysis of dependencies associated with cancer gene alterations (shown in Fig. 2f). These included 6 oncogenes (CTNNB1, EGFR, ERBB2, KRAS, PIK3CA and TERT) and 13 TSGs (APC, ARID1A, CDKN2A, CIC, FBXW7, NF1, PIK3R1, PTEN, RB1, RNF43, SMAD4, TP53 and HLA-B).


### next_gen_dependency_lineage_tests.csv
In the analysis of dependencies associated with cancer gene alterations (shown in Fig. 2f), we tested if the observed association between the cancer gene alteration and dependency was consistently beyond the effects caused by lineage-bias of the cancer gene alteration. Specifically, we computed the differences in mean dependency between NextGen screens in each lineage and the remaining (i.e., out-of-lineage) NextGen screens, only considering lineages which have at least 5 models screened (breast, CNS/brain, colorectal, esophagus/stomach, pancreas and prostate). If the dependency difference observed across the two classes of models distinguished by the presence of a cancer gene alteration was outside the range of all dependency differences observed across all lineages, then we deemed the dependency-biomarker relationship to be driven by the cancer gene alteration. Otherwise, the dependency pattern was attributed to lineage effects. This spreadsheet shows the mean dependency difference between in-lineage vs out-of-lineage groups for each lineage:gene pair (n = 6 [lineage], 5536 [gene]). Also see “Identification of biomarker-associated dependencies” subsection of Methods.


### next_gen_vs_traditional_metaprogram_expression_tests.csv
For each of the 41 gene expression meta-programs identified by Gavish et al. (Nature, 2023 [PMID: 37258682]), the activity of the program was scored in the NextGen (n = 305) and lineage-matched traditional (n = 548) models. The activities of individual programs were then compared between the NextGen and traditional model groups both per lineage and with all the organoid lineages (i.e., all lineages except for CNS/brain) combined. The number of models (“NextGenN” and “TraditionalN”) and the mean expression scores (“NextGenMean” and “TraditionalMean”) for both model groups, as well as the difference in mean expression scores (“MeanDifference”) are indicated. Statistical significance of the difference was scored by two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test (“MannWhitneyPValue”) and FDR (adjusted P-value; “MannWhitneyFDR”) was calculated using the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure.


### onc_tsg_alteration_dependency_tests_full.csv
For oncogenes and TSGs that have sufficient number of NextGen models (n ≥ 5) with relevant alteration (hotspot mutation and/or amplification for oncogenes and damaging mutation and/or deletion for TSGs) (n = 13 [oncogene], 38 [TSG]), gene dependencies were compared between altered and unaltered NextGen models (n = 147 [model], 5536 [gene]). Mean dependency difference as well as the statistical significance of the difference, assessed by two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test, were scored (“NextGenMeanDifference” and “NextGenMannWhitneyPValue”, respectively). The same comparison of gene dependency was conducted between the altered and unaltered traditional models (model n = 597) and the difference in mean dependency as well as the statistical significance of the difference were scored (“TraditionalMeanDifference” and “TraditionalMannWhitneyPValue”, respectively). Note that in the plot shown in Fig. 2f, only the oncogenes and TSGs that have at least one NextGen model with a hotspot mutation (n = 6 [oncogene], 13 [TSG]) and dependencies in the three strong dependency classes (“NextGenDepClass” strongly selective, high variance and pan-essential; gene n = 3786) were included.


### oncogene_gof_alteration_matrix.csv
For each of the cancer genes (oncogenes and tumor suppressor genes [TSGs]; annotated by the OncoKB; https://www.oncokb.org) (gene n = 741), the NextGen models (n = 147) and lineage-matched traditional models (n = 597) were classified based on the presence or absence of the gain-of-function (GOF) alteration of the gene (hotspot mutation and/or amplification). The values indicate the presence/absence of GOF alteration: 0 = absent; 1 = present. This made a basis for selecting oncogenes with sufficient representation of GOF models among the NextGen cohort (see “nextgen_cancer_gene_annotations.csv”) as well as the analysis for dependencies associated with oncogene GOF (Figs. 2f,g).


### organoids_vs_2d_gene_dependency_tail_enrichment_tests_full.csv
The top 100 genes in either extreme of the distribution of mean dependency differences (3D organoids [n = 94] vs lineage-matched 2D models [n = 433]; gene n = 16691 excluding common essential genes) were combined to form a set of 200 genes with differential dependencies. Gene sets from the Hallmark and KEGG legacy collections were tested for enrichment among this set of 200 differential dependency genes using a hypergeometric test (or one-sided Fisher’s exact test). The number of differential dependency genes within each gene set (“OverlapSize”), the size of the gene set after removing essential genes (“EffectiveSetSize”), the original size of the gene set (“OriginalSetSize”), as well as the odds ratio and P-value from the hypergeometric test (“OddsRatio” and “FishersPValue”, respectively), are indicated. Only gene sets with EffectiveSetSize between 101 and 249 were included in the further analysis. The scores shown here underlie the plot shown in Fig. 5a. Also see “Differential dependency analysis between 3D organoids and 2D models” subsection of Methods. 


### organoids_vs_2d_gene_dependency_tests_full.csv
Gene dependencies were compared between the NextGen models that were grown as 3D organoids (n = 94) vs lineage-matched 2D adherent models (n = 433) (gene n = 18159). Mean dependency difference (“MeanDifference”), the statistical significance of the difference assessed by two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test (“MannWhitneyPValue”), as well as the adjusted P-values calculated by the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure (“MannWhitneyFDR”) were scored. Common essential genes, which were removed from the analysis of differential dependency genes (see “organoids_vs_2d_gene_dependency_tail_enrichment_tests_full.csv”), were also labeled (“DropEssential”). This spreadsheet was used to compile the list of 200 differential dependency genes between 2D and 3D models (see “organoids_vs_2d_gene_dependency_tail_enrichment_tests_full.csv”).


### organoids_vs_2d_top_geneset_similarity_matrix.csv
For the nine gene sets enriched for differential dependency genes (see Fig. 5a), their similarity was assessed by calculating the pairwise overlap coefficient, whose values are shown in this spreadsheet. Gene sets with pairwise overlap similarity > 0.3 (KEGG_FOCAL_ADHESION and KEGG_REGULATION_OF_ACTIN_CYTOSKELETON, HALLMARK_G2M_CHECKPOINT and  HALLMARK_E2F_TARGETS) were merged to form functional clusters, resulting in two merged gene sets ("Adhesion/Cytoskeleton" and "Cell Cycle") and five ("Lipid Metabolism," "mTORC1," "OxPhos," "UV," and "SLE") gene sets that remained separate (see Extended Data Fig. 8h).


### paralog_dependency_tests_full.csv
For all the paralog pairs listed on the Ensembl genome browser 113 (https://www.ensembl.org/) with both genes scored for expression and dependency in the NextGen models (paralog pair n = 60527), we computed the Pearson correlation between the expression scores of one of the genes and dependency scores of the other across the NextGen models (model n = 146) (“NextGenPearsonR”). Statistical significance of the correlation was assessed by two-tailed Pearson correlation (“NextGenPValue”). The expression-dependency correlation of the same paralog pair was also scored in the data from the lineage-matched traditional model cohort (n = 572) and recorded (“TraditionalPearsonR”). Note that only genes in the three strong dependency classes (“NextGenDepClass” strongly selective, high variance and pan-essential; gene n = 3786) were plotted in Fig. 2d.


### pdac_classical_dependency_tests_full.csv
The correlation between gene dependency and expression of the PDAC-classical/MUC program across the cohort of the NextGen organoid models were scored for all the genes (n = 108 [model], 18159 [gene]) (“OrganoidPearsonR”). Statistical significance of the correlation was assessed by two-tailed Pearson correlation (“OrganoidPValue”). The membership in the MSigDB “GOBP: WNT_SIGNALING_PATHWAY” is also indicated (“WntSignaling”). Note that only genes in the three strong dependency classes based on the organoid screen data (“OrganoidDepClass” strongly selective, high variance and pan-essential; gene n = 3521) were plotted in Fig. 4d.


### tda_dependency_classification.csv
The dependency profiles of individual genes across the NextGen models were classified into either of the five classes (Non-dependency, Weakly selective, Strongly selective, High variance and Pan-dependency) based primarily on the strategy described in a prior publication (Krill-Burger et al. Genome Biol, 2023 [PMID: 37612728]) (also see “Classification of dependency profiles” subsection of Methods) (n = 18159 [gene], 147 [model]) (“NextGenClass”). The same classification strategy was also applied to the screens of the NextGen organoids (i.e., NextGen models out of the CNS/brain lineage) (n = 108 [organoid]) (“OrganoidsClass”). The variance of dependency scores as well as the dependency rank of each gene in their 90th percentile least depleted model (used for identifying “Pan-dependency” class; also see Dempster et al., bioRxiv, 2019; https://doi.org/10.1101/720243 for the details) were also recorded based both on the NextGen models dependency scores and organoid dependency scores (“NextGen90%ileLineRank” and “Organoids90%ileLineRank”, respectively).


### tumor_suppressor_lof_alteration_matrix.csv
For each of the cancer genes (oncogenes and tumor suppressor genes [TSGs]; annotated by the OncoKB; https://www.oncokb.org) (gene n = 741), the NextGen models (n = 147) and lineage-matched traditional models (n = 597) were classified based on the presence or absence of the loss-of-function (LOF) alteration of the gene (damaging mutation and/or deletion). The values indicate the presence/absence of LOF alteration: 0 = absent; 1 = present. This made a basis for selecting TSGs with sufficient representation of LOF models among the NextGen cohort (see “nextgen_cancer_gene_annotations.csv”) as well as the analysis for dependencies associated with TSG LOF (Fig. 2f).

PAPER PMC13581617.xml
SECTION CRISPR–Cas9 screening
Detailed protocols including process diagrams and example data for generating Cas9-expressing organoid cultures and CRISPR–Cas9 library transduction in organoid are published on protocols.io93,94. Stable Cas9-expressing organoids were generated using lentiCas9-Blast (Addgene 52962) with polybrene (8 μg ml−1). Following overnight incubation, medium was replaced with complete medium containing Y-27632 (2.5 μM), and blasticidin (Invivogen, ant-bl-1, 10 mg ml−1) selection was applied 120 h after transduction. Cas9 activity was measured using a fluorescent reporter assay (Addgene 67982 and 67981)95. Only organoid lines with activity of 75% or more were selected for single guide RNA (sgRNA) library transduction.
Two genome-wide CRISPR–Cas9 sgRNA libraries were used. The Human CRISPR Library Yusa v.1.1 (ref. 3), containing 100,086 sgRNAs that target 18,009 genes (with 5–10 sgRNAs per gene) and 1,004 non-targeting sgRNAs; and the Minimal Genome-Wide Human CRISPR–Cas9 Library (MinLibCas9, Addgene 164896)41, which includes a selected sgRNA primarily drawn from Yusa v1.1 comprising 37,522 sgRNAs that target 18,761 genes (with 2 optimal sgRNAs per gene) and 200 non-targeting sgRNAs (Extended Data Fig. 8a). Lentiviral volume required for multiplicity of infection of 0.3 was determined by titration, and transduction efficiency assessed by BFP flow cytometry.
A total of 12.5 × 108 (Yusa v1.1) or 3.3 × 107 (MinLibCas9) cells were transduced in triplicate using the same batch of Cas9-transduced organoids. No significant batch effects were observed across independent batches of Cas9-expressing organoid lines (Extended Data Fig. 8b). Cells were infected with the lentiviral-packaged whole-genome sgRNA library (for 100× coverage), in medium containing polybrene (8 μg ml−1), and Y-27632 (2.5 μM). Following overnight incubation, cells were plated in fresh medium in a 5% suspension. Transduction efficiency (target of 30%) was confirmed on day 6. Screens were maintained under puromycin selection (Invivogen, ant-pr-1, 10 mg ml−1) for a further 16 days (21 days in screen in total). A final selection efficiency of at least 60% was required for the screen to pass. At the end of the screen approximately 2.5 × 107 cells were collected, pelleted, and stored at −80 °C for downstream processing.
SECTION CRISPR screen data processing
CRISPR screens performed using the Yusa v1.1 (ref. 3) and MinLibCas9 (ref. 41) libraries were harmonized by restricting analyses to shared sgRNAs, enabling integrated downstream processing. Quality control procedures, adapted from established cell line screening pipelines3, were applied to assess replicate concordance, sgRNA representation and classifier performance in distinguishing essential from non-essential genes. Low-quality replicates and organoids failing predefined quality control criteria were excluded prior to further analysis. Read counts were normalized and corrected for copy-number effects using CRISPRcleanR (v3.0.1)96, followed by batch correction across libraries97. Gene-level fitness effects were estimated using BAGEL (v2)45 with curated reference gene sets, and LFC values were scaled relative to essential and non-essential controls to facilitate cross-organoid comparability. Full details of sgRNA selection, quality control thresholds, normalization, batch correction, statistical procedures and final selection of models are provided in the Supplementary Information.
SECTION CRISPR screen processed data analysis
SECTION Differential dependency analysis in all gastrointestinal, COAD/READ and ESCA organoids
We conducted an analysis to identify genes with the greatest dependency variability across organoids within each cancer type and to highlight context-specific dependencies. The analysis proceeded as follows:
Following these filtering steps, for each remaining gene, we calculated the difference in LFCs (delta LFC) between organoids where the gene was depleted and those where it was not and tested the statistical significance with a Fisher’s exact test. Only genes with a FDR-adjusted P value < 0.05 were considered differentially dependent (7,086 for gastrointestinal, 5,103 for COAD/READ and 4,082 for ESCA; Supplementary Table 7).
PAPER PMC13664187.xml
SECTION 2.10.1. Retrospective Evaluation of Gene Ranking Within Organoids
A retrospective secondary analysis evaluated whether the frozen expression-only predictions ranked the 20 genes within individual organoids in agreement with measured CRISPR outcomes. This secondary analysis was defined after the external outcomes were available and was not a new blinded validation. It retained all 20 targets and all 94 Broad and 148 Sanger organoids; Tiriac was excluded because measured dependency outcomes were unavailable. No predictions, models, features, hyperparameters, or targets were changed, and the original target-wise primary analysis was retained.
For each organoid, we calculated Spearman correlation across its 20 predicted and 20 observed scores, using average ranks for ties, and summarized the correlations by their arithmetic mean. Lower scores denoted stronger measured or predicted dependency. The main comparator assigned every organoid the same per-gene mean of the observed outcomes across all 852 2D training models, with each training model weighted equally. This fixed ranking tested whether expression predictions improved on gene-level average dependency. We also reused the archived lineage-only predictions: the ridge reference in Broad and patient-weighted 2D lineage means in Sanger. No external outcomes were used to fit either comparator (Table S17).
To examine a short candidate list, we calculated the overlap between the three lowest predicted scores and the three lowest measured scores, divided by three, within each organoid. The cohort mean was expressed as a percentage. Ties at the selection boundary were to be resolved by the fixed target identifiers; none occurred. Expression-minus-comparator differences were calculated within the same organoids for both the full-ranking correlation and top-three overlap (Table S18). These endpoints assess agreement with measured CRISPR score ordering within the selected panel, not cancer selectivity, cross-gene calibration, drug response, or clinical benefit.
We estimated 95% percentile CIs using 10,000 paired bootstrap resamples: organoid models sampled within observed lineage strata for Broad, and donor clusters sampled with all their organoids retained together for Sanger. The Sanger main estimate remained organoid-weighted. A donor-level sensitivity analysis first averaged the predicted and observed 20-gene vectors within each of 144 donors, then recalculated both endpoints with equal donor weights and a 10,000-replicate donor bootstrap (Table S19). Seeds were 20260928, 20260929, and 20260930 for Broad, Sanger, and donor-mean sensitivity, respectively. All correlations were defined. Intervals were conditional on the frozen models and fixed panel; they did not include model-training or gene-panel selection uncertainty. The secondary comparisons were not adjusted for multiplicity and were interpreted as exploratory.
SECTION 3.3. Exploratory Transfer of the Locked Model to Broad-94 Organoids
The finalized expression-only deployment model was applied to 94 Broad NextGen organoids without retraining, cohort-specific feature selection, or recalibration. All 300 locked expression features were available for every target, yielding 100% feature coverage. The 94 × 20 prediction matrix was written and SHA-256-sealed before alignment with the observed 3D Gene Effect outcomes. Because Broad-94 had been accessible in earlier methodological work, this analysis remained an exploratory 3D transfer assessment rather than an independent external validation. Panel-wide, target-level, bootstrap, and permutation results are shown in Figure 4a–d; full results and lineage sensitivity analyses are provided in Table S12.
As shown in Figure 4a,c,d, the macro target-wise Spearman correlation in Broad-94 was 0.1518 (95% lineage-stratified bootstrap CI, 0.1019–0.2006). Eighteen of 20 target correlations were positive, and the median target correlation was 0.1628. The Broad-94 macro correlation was 0.2576 lower than the locked 2D holdout value. In the within-lineage permutation analysis, an association at least as large as the observed value occurred rarely (one-sided p = 0.00080), supporting partial but attenuated transfer of rank information.
The lineage-adjusted macro Spearman correlation was 0.1080, whereas the lineage-only reference yielded 0.0951. The paired expression-only-minus-lineage-only difference was +0.0567 (95% CI, 0.0040–0.1106). Thus, lineage composition did not fully explain the exploratory association, although it accounted for part of the observed signal.
At the target level, the highest correlations were observed for ERBB3 (ρ = 0.3489), CCNE1 (ρ = 0.3456), CTNNB1 (ρ = 0.3009), E2F3 (ρ = 0.2679), and YAP1 (ρ = 0.2614) (Figure 4b; Table S12). MAPK1 (ρ = −0.0307) and STK11 (ρ = −0.1010) were the only targets with negative correlations. After adjustment of the 20 target-level permutation p values, no target met the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR threshold of q < 0.05; the lowest adjusted value was q = 0.0640 for CCNE1. Accordingly, Broad-94 was interpreted at the fixed-panel level rather than as confirmatory evidence for individual targets.
Among lineage groups containing at least seven models, macro correlations ranged from −0.0099 to 0.2786. Leave-one-lineage-out estimates ranged from 0.1202 to 0.1832, and the pooled association remained positive after every lineage exclusion. The original estimates used model-level resampling. The subsequent donor audit and sensitivity analyses below address the identified shared donor, while retaining uncertainty about unresolved donor relationships.
The donor audit identified one confirmed same-donor pair among 25 models with donor information; 69 models remained unresolved (Table S22). Omitting ACH-002883 or ACH-002884 gave macro target-wise correlations of 0.1516 and 0.1539, respectively, compared with the original 0.1518. Under the conditional full-cohort bootstrap, the expression-only estimate was 0.1518 (0.1009 to 0.2007) and its paired difference from lineage-only prediction was 0.0567 (0.0027 to 0.1081) (estimate and 95% CI; Table S23). These findings quantify sensitivity to the identified pair, conditional on treating the unresolved models as separate units.
In the 25-model donor-documented subset, the expression-only macro correlation was 0.0741 (−0.0154 to 0.1618), with an expression-minus-lineage difference of −0.0571 (−0.1502 to 0.0305) under source-specific donor-cluster resampling. Both intervals included zero, so this subset did not establish superiority over lineage. Averaging the vectors within the 24 source-specific donor groups gave 0.0727 (−0.0195 to 0.1629) for expression-only prediction. This subset is not a representative replacement for Broad-94; changes relative to the full cohort can reflect sample and lineage composition as well as grouping. Paired within-subset model-versus-cluster intervals and secondary ranking sensitivities are provided in Tables S23 and S24. Incomplete donor ascertainment prevents these analyses from verifying full-cohort independence or providing a fully donor-adjusted effective sample size.
SECTION 3.7. Retrospective Gene Ranking Within Individual Organoids
Using the unchanged 20-gene predictions, the mean within-organoid Spearman correlation was 0.3428 (95% CI, 0.3026 to 0.3826) in Broad-94 and 0.4294 (95% CI, 0.3926 to 0.4645) in Sanger-148 (Table S17). The corresponding fixed 2D-mean comparator estimates were 0.2057 and 0.1995. Paired expression-minus-fixed-mean differences were +0.1371 (95% CI, 0.0987 to 0.1780) and +0.2299 (95% CI, 0.1981 to 0.2613), respectively. These estimates address gene ordering within each organoid and are not directly comparable to the original macro target-wise correlations of 0.1518 and 0.1015.
The archived lineage-only predictions yielded mean within-organoid correlations of 0.2745 in Broad and 0.3884 in Sanger. Expression-minus-lineage differences were +0.0684 (95% CI, 0.0334 to 0.1032) and +0.0411 (95% CI, 0.0167 to 0.0656), respectively. Thus, full-panel gene ranking showed a modest retrospective gain over lineage-only prediction in both cohorts (Table S17). This finding does not change the uncertainty in the original Sanger target-wise expression-minus-lineage difference.
Top-three overlap was 34.04% in Broad and 42.79% in Sanger, compared with 34.75% and 35.36% for lineage-only prediction (Table S18). Paired expression-minus-lineage differences were −0.71 percentage points (95% CI, −4.96 to 3.19) in Broad and +7.43 percentage points (95% CI, 4.28 to 10.74) in Sanger. The Broad interval included zero; superiority for selecting the three strongest measured dependencies was therefore not demonstrated across both cohorts.
Averaging the 20-gene vectors within each Sanger donor before evaluation gave a mean within-donor correlation of 0.4338 (95% CI, 0.3986 to 0.4675). The paired difference from lineage-only prediction was +0.0402 (95% CI, 0.0157 to 0.0652). Mean top-three overlap was 42.82%, with an expression-minus-lineage difference of +6.94 percentage points (95% CI, 4.17 to 9.95; Table S19). These results were consistent with the organoid-weighted analysis. All within-organoid findings remain retrospective and restricted to the selected 20-gene panel.
PAPER PMC13581591.xml
SECTION Genome-wide CRISPR screening of organoids
After initial expansion and collection of viable stocks and pellets (used for fingerprinting and omics profiling; Extended Data Fig. 3), the organoids were tested for blasticidin sensitivity to identify the minimal concentration of blasticidin that gives less than 1% viability of uninfected cells in 10–14 days. Fourteen organoid models were screened as adherent cultures on Matrigel-coated plates, whereas the rest of the organoids were screened in 3D Matrigel dome cultures. To select the culture conditions for organoid screens, each of the organoid models was subjected to a growth format assay by seeding 50,000 cells into a well of a 24-well non-tissue-culture-treated plate (Corning, 351147) either as a Matrigel dome (75% Matrigel, 50 μl per dome) or on top of a Matrigel coat, referred to as either dome or coat format, respectively. The coated wells were prepared by first adding 1% Matrigel in ice-cold PBS to 50% of the standard filling volume of the well and allowing it to solidify by incubating overnight at 37 °C. Before cell plating, the PBS–Matrigel mixture was aspirated, leaving the Matrigel coat behind, and the cell suspension was added on top of the coat. The growth of cells in the dome and coat formats was compared after 1 week of culture via a viability assay with CellTiter-Glo 3D (3D CTG; Promega, G9683). Models with at least 30% viability on coat relative to dome format were propagated and screened in the coat format. This process enabled us to screen these models in a similar manner to traditional adherent cell lines, but with a thin layer of matrix support, thus decreasing the amount of required Matrigel by approximately tenfold.
Subsequently, all organoids were infected with lentivirus expressing enAsCas12a (Addgene, 136476). For this infection, 1.5 × 106 cells were exposed to the lentivirus in a 24-well ULA plate (Corning, 3473) in the presence of 8 µg ml–1 polybrene and 10 µM ROCK inhibitor. After 18 h, the cells were collected by centrifugation and washed once with PBS to remove the virus, and cells were then plated in Matrigel domes at 5 × 104 cells per dome. At 24 h after seeding the cells in the Matrigel domes, the medium was replaced with selection medium containing blasticidin at 30 µg ml–1. A 3D CTG assay was carried out 10–14 days after infection to ensure efficient elimination of uninfected cells with blasticidin and to measure the infection rate from the enAsCas12a virus. Cells were maintained at a constant blasticidin (as determined using a blasticidin sensitivity assay) concentration throughout the screening process.
Cas12a activity assessment was conducted as previously described65 (a detailed protocol is available as ‘Cas9/Cas12a Activity Assay’ at the Broad Institute Genetic Perturbation Platform (GPP) web portal: https://portals.broadinstitute.org/gpp/public/resources/protocols) using two different types of lentivirus, both of which express GFP, a sgRNA (or sgRNAs) against GFP and the puromycin-resistance gene. One type of these virus expresses two GFP-targeting sgRNA sequences optimized for enAsCas12a (pRDA_221; Addgene, 169142), whereas the other virus type expresses a GFP-targeting sgRNA sequence optimized for spCas9 (pXPR-047; Addgene, 107145), the latter of which served as a negative control. Cells were infected with these two different virus types separately as described above and selected with puromycin for 4 days. GFP expression was measured in the FITC channel by flow cytometry (CytoFLEX LX, Beckman Coulter) 14 days after pRDA_221/pXPR_047 infection with a Zombie Aqua viability stain (BioLegend, 423102) applied to gate out dead cells. Using the non-infection control (NIC) as a negative control, GFP positivity was determined for both the pRDA_221 and pXPR_047 infected cells. Cas activity was calculated using the following formula: (GFP (%) of pXPR_047 – GFP (%) of pRDA_221)/(GFP (%) of pXPR_047).
Cell lines with Cas activity above 70% were selected for screening. However, some lines were screened with activity as low as 44% if they showed sufficiently rapid growth of at least one population doubling per week.
Library titrations were carried out to determine the volume of sgRNA library virus required to achieve an infection efficiency of around 50%. Infections were conducted on 24-well ULA plates, with each well containing 5 × 105 cells in 600 µl medium with 4–8 µg ml–1 polybrene, 10 µM ROCK inhibitor and 50–200 µl library virus. The plate was incubated at 37 °C for 18 h, followed by collection of the cells by centrifugation and a wash with PBS to remove the virus. Cells were subsequently plated in Matrigel domes on a 24-well plate. Puromycin (3 µg ml–1) was added 48 h after virus infection. After 4 days of selection, infection efficiency was determined using a 3D CTG cell viability assay.
For the screens, cells were first infected using the same protocol as for library titration, but only with the virus volume selected to give an infection rate of about 50%. The number of cells used for infection was determined such that sufficient number of cells survived puromycin selection to produce at least 500× representation of the library. Before July 2022, the screens were performed using either the C or D versions of the Humagne library (Addgene, 172650 and 172651) at full representation for each library. By contrast, screens after July 2022 were performed in two technical replicates at half representation using a combined C and D library to enable measurement of replicate correlation. After 18 h of incubation with virus in ULA plates, the virus was washed out, and infected cells were plated at 5 × 104 cells per dome. To test infection efficiency, we seeded four NIC domes and four infected domes in a 24-well plate (one dome per well). After 24 h, the medium was replaced with the selection medium containing puromycin for the infected plates and half of the domes (two NIC domes and two infected domes) for the infection efficiency assay. Puromycin was washed out and blasticidin treatment was resumed on day 6, when a 3D CTG viability measurement for the infection efficiency assay was conducted to estimate actual screen representation. Screens with less than 200× representation were excluded. The medium was replaced on days 13 and 20, and the samples were passaged on day 9 and optionally on day 17 depending on the confluence of the model, which was determined by visual inspection, each time maintaining minimum target representation. All the cells were collected and frozen as pellets on day 23.
gDNA samples were prepared from the screen pellets using a KingFisher Apex System (Thermo Fisher, 5400940). After preparing gDNA samples, the DNA concentration of each sample was measured using a Qubit4 Fluorometer (Thermo Fisher, Q33238), and the presence of library DNA was confirmed by performing PCR with a set of library-specific primers. gDNA samples were subsequently submitted for library DNA amplification and sequencing to the GPP at the Broad Institute. PCR and sequencing were performed in accordance with the ‘sgRNA/shRNA/ORF PCR for Illumina Sequencing’ protocol available at the Broad Institute GPP web portal (https://portals.broadinstitute.org/gpp/public/resources/protocols). DNA samples were sequenced at a scale matched with the size of the screen: 68 µg gDNA per replicate for screens with 500× representation and 134 µg gDNA per replicate for screens with 1,000× representation.
SECTION Genome-wide CRISPR screening of NextGen CNS tumours
The pipeline for genome-wide CRISPR screens of the NextGen CNS tumour models was adapted in part from previous work66 and is composed of the same processes to what is described above in the section ‘Genome-wide CRISPR screening of organoids’ except for the following differences.
CNS models were tested for the growth format and seeding density that resulted in optimal growth rates and for polybrene sensitivity, which was used at a concentration that resulted in >85% survival. For growth testing, cells were plated in both Matrigel-coated and ULA 6-well tissue culture plates. Specifically, cells were seeded in duplicate at densities of 100,000, 200,000 and 300,000 cells per well for both the Matrigel-coated plates and ULA plates. Cells were allowed to grow for 7 days, with a medium change performed at day 3 or 4 as needed. After 7 days, cells from all the conditions were counted and a doubling time was calculated for each condition. The condition that gave the lowest doubling time while maintaining high cell viability was used for the screen. Blasticidin and puromycin were used at a standardized concentration of 50 µg ml–1 and 3 µg ml–1, respectively. For enAsCas12a transduction, 1.5 × 106 cells were exposed to lentivirus expressing enAsCas12ain a 24-well ULA plate in the presence of the optimized concentration of polybrene. At 18 h after infection, cells were washed with PBS to remove the virus and the cells were plated in the optimal growth format at 2× the optimal seeding density. Subsequently, cells were selected with blasticidin. A CellTiter-Glo cell viability assay (CTG; Promega, G7572) was carried out 14 days after infection to ensure efficient elimination of uninfected cells with blasticidin and to measure the infection rate from the enAsCas12a virus.
For library titration, infections were conducted on 24-well ULA plates, with each well containing 1.5 × 106 cells in 600 µl medium, the optimized concentration of polybrene and 50–200 µl library virus. The volume of virus required for an infection efficiency of about 50% was determined as described above in ‘Genome-wide CRISPR screening of organoids’. Blasticidin treatment was resumed on day 7 instead of day 6 after infection, and a CTG viability measurement for the infection efficiency assay was performed on the same day to estimate actual screen representation. Medium was replaced on days 10 and 17, and the samples were passaged on days 7 and 14, each time maintaining minimum target representation. All cells were collected and frozen as pellets on day 21, from which gDNA samples were prepared, and the library DNA was amplified by PCR and sequenced.
SECTION Genome-wide CRISPR screens of cancer cell lines
The original source for all the cell lines is available at the DepMap portal (https://DepMap.org/portal). The CRISPR screens of the traditional cancer cell lines with the spCas9 sgRNA libraries (Avana and KY) were carried out as previously described67,68.
For the CRISPR screens of the traditional cancer cell lines with the enAsCas12a sgRNA library (Humagne-CD), the cell lines were first tested for doubling time and cell size. Blasticidin and puromycin were used at a standard concentration of 40 μg ml–1 and 2 μg ml–1, respectively. To infect adherent cells with lentivirus expressing enAsCas12a (Addgene, 136476), 5 × 106 cells were exposed to the virus in a T175 flask (Corning, 354487) in the presence of 4 µg ml–1 polybrene. For suspension cells, 1.8 × 107 cells were exposed to the virus in a 12-well plate (Corning, 3513) in the presence of 4 µg ml–1 polybrene, which was followed by centrifugation at 930g, 37 °C for 2 h. One day after infection, cells were washed with PBS to remove the virus and cells were then plated at around 50% confluence. Blasticidin was added to the medium 2–3 days after infection. A CTG assay was carried out after 3 days of blasticidin selection to ensure efficient elimination of uninfected cells with blasticidin and to estimate the infection rate from the enAsCas12a virus. Cells were maintained in blasticidin for at least 10 days after transduction and throughout the screening process. After enCas12a transduction and blasticidin selection, enCas12a activity was assessed as described in the section ‘Genome-wide CRISPR screening of organoids’.
Library titrations were performed in 12-well tissue-culture-treated plates, with each well containing 1.5 × 106 cells in 2 ml medium, 4 μg ml–1 polybrene and 0, 10, 25, 75, 200 or 500 μl virus. These 12-well plates were spun at 930g, 37 °C for 2 h, after which an additional 2 ml medium was added to each well for a final volume of 4 ml per well. After 24 h of infection, the cells in each well of the 12-well infection plate were split into 2 wells of a 6-well plate with 0 and 2 μg ml–1 puromycin in fresh medium. An optimal seeding density was chosen such that the unselected population reached <70% confluence after selection. After 3 days of selection, infection efficiency for a given virus volume was determined as the ratio of live cells in the puromycin-containing well (2 μg ml–1) to the number of live cells in the puromycin non-containing well. A linear regression of the virus volume to the transduction efficiency was performed to estimate the volume of virus required to transduce 50% of the population.
The remaining procedures of the screen were the same as for the procedures described above in the section ‘Genome-wide CRISPR screening of organoids’ except for the following modifications. For library infection, after 24 h of incubation with virus in 12-well plates, the virus was washed out with PBS and infected cells were plated with puromycin at an appropriate seeding density and flask size. After 72 h of selection, puromycin was washed out and blasticidin treatment was resumed. Screens with less than 250× representation or at an infection efficiency of higher than 60% were excluded. Cells were passaged every 3–6 days depending on the confluence of the model, as determined by visual inspection, each time maintaining minimum target representation. All cells were collected and frozen as pellets on day 22–23.
SECTION Genome-wide CRISPR screening data processing and quality control evaluation
All screens were initially evaluated for sequencing quality and selected to have a mean of at least 185 reads per gene. Naive gene effect scores were calculated by taking the log of the ratio of normalized reads for each gene in screened samples to that of normalized reads for each gene in the corresponding plasmid library. For screens using the two Humagne C and D libraries, the log fold change scores of libraries were averaged together. An ROC-AUC was calculated on the basis of per cent of true positive hits (essential genes with log fold change below –1) for every possible false discovery rate. An NNMD was calculated for each screen by taking the difference between the average log fold changes for essential and non-essential genes and dividing by the standard deviation of the non-essential genes69. Screens that had an NNMD greater than –1.25 or a correlation between replicates less than 0.19 were excluded from downstream analyses. Chronos scores were then calculated using the open-source code Chronos (v.2.0.8; https://github.com/broadinstitute/chronos) as previously described23 for each type of guide library (Avana spCas9, KY spCas9 or Humagne CD enAsCas12a) independently. To mitigate batch effects between guide libraries, the mean Chronos score per gene in individual libraries was regularized towards the global mean across all libraries. On the DepMap portal, the file ‘ScreenGeneEffect’ contains Chronos gene effect estimates for each individual screen, with library effects corrected out whereas organoid-specific effects are preserved. Beginning with the 26Q1 release, a similar strategy will be used to preserve organoid-specific effects in ‘CRISPRGeneEffect’, which integrates all screens of the same model into a model-indexed table that can be used with portal tools.
SECTION Differential dependency analysis between 3D organoids and 2D models
To identify functional classes that are enriched for genes exhibiting differing degrees of dependency between 2D models (screen results from traditional adherent models that are lineage-matched with 3D organoids; n = 433) and 3D organoids (n = 94), we ranked all genes (n = 16,691; excluding common essential genes) according to their mean difference in dependency score between organoid models and lineage-matched adherent models. The genes with the 100 most extreme differences in each direction were combined into one set. Gene sets from the Hallmark and KEGG legacy collections, which contain 101–249 member genes that were ranked in this analysis, were tested for enrichment among this set of 200 genes using a hypergeometric test (or one-sided Fisher’s exact test). The gene sets that achieved significance at q < 0.05 were compared for similarity using the overlap coefficient:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
                                                  \usepackage{amsmath}
                                                  \usepackage{wasysym}
                                                  \usepackage{amsfonts}
                                                  \usepackage{amssymb}
                                                  \usepackage{amsbsy}
                                                  \usepackage{mathrsfs}
                                                  \usepackage{upgreek}
                                                  \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
                                                  \begin{document}$${\rm{o}}{\rm{v}}{\rm{e}}{\rm{r}}{\rm{l}}{\rm{a}}{\rm{p}}(A,B)=\frac{|A\cap B|}{min(A,B)}$$\end{document}overlap(A,B)=|A∩B|min(A,B)
Gene sets with pairwise overlap similarity >0.3 were identified as belonging to the same functional cluster (Extended Data Fig. 8h). Clusters were successively combined using their original component gene sets until all clusters contain no gene sets having similarity >0.3 with any gene sets belonging to a different cluster.
SECTION Lentiviral production for the minipool screen and its validation
Lentiviral production was conducted using 293FT cells (Thermo Fisher, R70007) in accordance with the ‘Lentiviral Production in Flasks’ protocol available at the Broad Institute GPP web portal (https://portals.broadinstitute.org/gpp/public/resources/protocols). In brief, the lentiviral particles were generated by the co-transfecting the lentiviral plasmid with a packaging plasmid (psPAX2; 12260, Addgene) and VSV-G envelope (pMD2.G; Addgene, 12259) into 293FT cells using PEIpro transfection reagent (Polyplus, 101000033). The medium was replaced 12 h after transfection, and the virus-containing medium was collected after 36–48 h.
SECTION Library preparation for minipool screens
For the minipool screens, to evaluate the effects of growth formats and culture media on gene essentiality, we decided to select test genes from nine different functional classes. Most of these gene classes are related to the regulation of adhesion–cytoskeleton, lipid metabolism or other processes highlighted in the systematic comparison of genome-wide CRISPR screen data between 2D models and 3D organoids (Fig. 5a,b). The following gene classes were included: integrin (genes encoding an integrin α or β subunit); ECM adhesion (adhesome, https://adhesome.org/); actin regulation (MSigDB gene set and KEGG regulation of actin cytoskeleton); adherens junction (MSigDB gene set and KEGG adherens junction); tight junction (MSigDB gene set and KEGG tight junction); lipid metabolism (a union of two MSigDB gene sets, Hallmark cholesterol homeostasis and Reactome metabolism of lipids); WNT signalling (MSigDB gene set and GOBP WNT signalling pathway); PI3K–mTOR signalling (MSigDB gene set and Hallmark PI3K–mTOR signalling); cell cycle (MSigDB gene set and GOBP cell cycle); and other genes of particular interest (manual curation). To select 250 test genes from these 9 core functional gene classes, we used the dependency profiles of the gene between 2D models and 3D organoids to prioritize genes that exhibited selective enrichment of dependency in either of these model types. Many of these test genes belong to multiple different classes, and the patterns of membership of individual genes across the nine classes and the number of test genes that exhibit the specific pattern are presented in Extended Data Fig. 9a. We then added 650 non-dependency genes and 100 pan-dependency genes, genes that are universally essential across 2D models and 3D organoids, to compile a list of 1,000 genes (Supplementary Table 3). We subsequently designed 4 enAsCas12a sgRNA sequences for each of these 1,000 genes using the CRISPick web tool (https://portals.broadinstitute.org/gppx/crispick/public), which were assembled into 2 constructs (2 sgRNA sequences for the same gene per construct; 2 constructs per gene) of the pRDA_052 backbone (Addgene, 136474). The resulting library with 2,000 constructs was used for minipool screening.
SECTION Minipool screening
Before screening, models were tested for their growth in an alternative medium (1:1 OPAC organoid medium for 2D models and RPMI with 10% FBS for 3D organoids) and with alternative growth format (3D Matrigel dome growth for 2D models and 2D monolayer growth for 3D organoids) for at least two passages. Only the combination of medium type and growth format that enabled a comparable rate of growth with the original conditions (<2-fold difference in the doubling times) and reasonably rapid growth (doubling time <120 h), as well as the original conditions of growth format–culture medium pair, were used for the screening. Moreover, all the models selected for screening were subjected to titrations of library virus and puromycin to determine the dose of virus to achieve optimal infection rates and the concentration of puromycin to kill uninfected cells within 3 days, respectively.
For the screen, selected models were infected with the amount of library virus required for infecting 30% of the cells in accordance with the result of virus titration on day 0. The infected cells were selected with the concentration of puromycin determined in the puromycin titration between day 1 and day 4. On day 4, cells were seeded into up to 4 conditions (2D monolayer culture on plastic plates (plastic culture) with RPMI + 10% FBS (RPMI–FBS); plastic culture with 1:1 OPAC; 3D Matrigel dome culture (dome culture) with RPMI + 10% FBS; and dome culture with 1:1 OPAC), depending on how many conditions survived the above-mentioned selection criteria for the respective model. For plastic culture, 2 million cells were resuspended with 10 ml of the respective medium and seeded on a Falcon 100 mm TC-treated cell culture dish (Corning, 53003). For dome culture, 2 million cells were resuspended with 0.5 m of the medium used for the condition and subsequently mixed with 1.5 ml of Matrigel. Approximately 40 domes, each with 50 µl of Matrigel–cell suspension were placed on a 100 mm TC-treated culture dish, which was followed by the incubation of the plate at 37 °C for 30 min to allow the Matrigel to solidify. Subsequently, 10 ml of the culture medium was added before further incubation. We prepared two technical replicates per each condition.
These cultures were maintained until day 14 with refreshment of the medium every 3 days. If the culture showed signs of confluency (coverage of more than 80% of the bottom surface by the cells for monolayer culture on plastic plates and appearance of a dark spot in the middle of the domes for Matrigel dome culture), we passaged the cells instead of replacing the medium while keeping the number of cells after passaging to 2 million cells per dish. Trypsinization (with 0.25% trypsin and 0.1% EDTA (Corning, 25-053-CI)) was used for the passaging of cells in plastic cultures, whereas cells in dome cultures were passaged in accordance with the strategy described above in the section ‘Source and culture conditions of NextGen cancer models’. At day 14, cells were collected by trypsinization and counted. gDNA samples were prepared from these cells and the abundance of individual sgRNA sequences in the genome of these cells were analysed by PCR amplification of the sgRNA sequences, preparation of sequencing libraries from the PCR products and subsequent sequencing of these libraries as described in the section ‘Genome-wide CRISPR screening for gDNA isolation and sequencing’.
SECTION Minipool screen analysis
Minipool screens were evaluated for sequencing quality by having a total read count of at least 500,000 reads across replicates. Gene scores and NNMDs were computed in the same manner as the genome-wide screens. Replicate correlation was computed as the Pearson’s correlation coefficient over log fold changes in all non-control genes. Screens with NNMDs greater than –1.5 and replicate correlations less than 0.2 were excluded from downstream analyses. Screens with a single replicate were retained if their other metrics passed these quality control thresholds. Chronos scores were calculated as previously described23, with adjusted hyperparameters (smoothing regularization parameter = 0.3, kernel width = 5) for the smaller library size. Subsequently, we modelled the Chronos score Yg,k for each gene g and screen k with the generalized linear regression model:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
                                                  \usepackage{amsmath}
                                                  \usepackage{wasysym}
                                                  \usepackage{amsfonts}
                                                  \usepackage{amssymb}
                                                  \usepackage{amsbsy}
                                                  \usepackage{mathrsfs}
                                                  \usepackage{upgreek}
                                                  \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
                                                  \begin{document}$${Y}_{g,k}={\beta }_{0}+{\beta }_{d}{I}_{d}+{\beta }_{s}{I}_{s}+{\beta }_{{ds}}{I}_{d}{I}_{s}+\sum _{m\in M}{\beta }_{m}{I}_{m}$$\end{document}Yg,k=β0+βdId+βsIs+βdsIdIs+∑m∈MβmIm
The first term captures the baseline viability effect of knocking out gene g. The second and third terms capture the growth format effect and medium effect, where Id and Is are indicator variables representing the types of growth format (Matrigel dome culture (dome) versus monolayer culture on plastic plates (plastic)) and culture medium (serum-free OPAC medium versus serum-containing RPMI–FBS medium), respectively. The fourth term captures interaction effects between these two conditions, and the final term captures model effects to account for multiple screens in the same cell line. Differences in effect sizes across growth format and serum status were extracted as βd and βs, respectively. Significance values corresponding to the same coefficients were extracted from the P values calculated using two-sided linear regression t-test.
To assess the effects of growth format (or culture medium) on the core functional classes of gene targets included in our minipool library, we calculated the variance in βd (or βs) across members of the same class and compared it to the variance in βd (or βs) across all non-control genes outside of class. For each gene set, we tested whether the in-class variance was significantly greater than the out-of-class variance using one-tailed F-tests.

Overall, the clinical information associated with each organoid—including disease stage, risk factors and prior treatment—enhance the relevance of the biobank, enable rational model selection and support integration with external clinical datasets for deeper interpretation.
We completed quality-control-passed CRISPR–Cas9 screens on 162 unique organoid cultures from 5 tumour types (85 COAD/READ (exceeding the 63 cell lines screened in DepMap), 59 ESCA, 11 OV, 4 PAAD and 3 STAD; overall 85% success rate), using both MinLibCas9 and Yusa v1.1 libraries for 16 models (Extended Data Fig. 8 for screen quality control and Supplementary Table 4). Using the BAGEL2 method45, we identified a median of 1,440 fitness genes in each organoid (range 297–2,160; Fig. 4a and Supplementary Table 5), fewer than reported in cell lines (median = 2,507 for 930 cell lines9). The number of fitness genes was inversely proportional to the log fold change (LFC) standard deviation and other quality control metrics (Fig. 4b, Extended Data Fig. 8g,h and Supplementary Tables 4 and 6), indicating that greater variability in gene depletion negatively affects fitness gene identification. We applied rigorous quality control criteria to screens, including replicate concordance, clear discrimination of reference essential versus non-essential genes (area under receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) = 0.97 and area under precision-recall curve (AUPR) = 0.973) and good effect size (Fig. 4c,d and Supplementary Table 4), with results comparable to cell lines3,9 (AUROC = 0.92 and AUPR = 0.9). Our results demonstrate the feasibility of constructing organoid dependency maps, with the potential to uncover new biology.
a. Pathways enriched in gene set enrichment analysis (GSEA) of 97 organoid-specific core fitness genes. Essential, non-essential and genes untested in organoids owing to single guide RNA (sgRNA) selection were excluded, and compared with pan-cancer core fitness genes from cell lines. Dot size indicates the number of genes per pathway (gene names displayed), while colour represents the FDR-adjusted p-value after Fisher’s exact test. Only pathways with FDR < 10% are shown. BP: biological processes; KEGG: KEGG pathways; H: hallmarks. b. Annotation of organoid-specific core essential genes based on genetic constraint, phenotypic effect in mouse knock-outs and tissue specificity of gene expression. Light green indicates highly constrained with severe knockout phenotypes (new core fitness genes); red indicates low constraint with normal knockout phenotypes (cancer organoid selective core fitness genes). c. Scaled log fold change (LFC) values of example genes per category in cell lines (n = 930) and in organoids (n = 154). d. Volcano plot of statistically significant gene-biomarker associations. Dot colour indicates association significance (A > B > C), while shape corresponds to cancer type. Dashed lines indicate p-value cutoffs. e. Log fold change (LFC) profile of WRN with corresponding biomarker associations in colorectal adenocarcinoma (COAD/READ) organoid (n = 85). f. Same as panel e for MDM2. Only organoids with Nutlin-3a log IC50 values are shown (n = 49). g. Differences in CCNE1 LFCs based on the presence or absence of CCNE1 amplification in oesophageal adenocarcinoma (ESCA) organoids (n = 59). h. Correlation between INTS6 LFCs and INTS6L expression in all gastrointestinal organoids (n = 151). P-values and effect sizes from the linear regression model are provided in the text. All box plots display the median, first and third quartiles (boxes), and whiskers are 1.5x the interquartile range from the first and third quartiles.

     5	## The single question
     6	**Does measured format-bundle lability of a gene's dependency (Broad controlled panel) predict how badly that gene's dependency ranking transfers to independent Sanger organoids — or can any such association be bounded below decision relevance?**
     7	
     8	## Locked-at-G1 definitions (Lock 1 finalizes numerics, not identity)
     9	- **Lability (predictor, Broad Layer-A, frozen values):** for gene g, `L_g` = equal-weight mean over the **6 format-contrast models** of (mean over that model's pairs of **|Chronos_Dome − Chronos_Plastic|**), medium held fixed within pair. Bundle = Matrigel-dome vs plastic. (Operator per sol-xhigh unstick; no within-model cancellation.)
    10	- **External transfer loss (response, Sanger Layer-C):** cohort = **148 organoids / 144 donors** (CRISPR_available, donor-level Broad-disjoint via `overlap_Broad_DepMap` at donor level; independently matches S3's 148/144).     11	  For organoid o and gene g: within-organoid rank `R_og` of LFC across the confirmatory gene set (rank 1 = strongest dependency). Consensus 2D rank `R0_g` from Broad `AdherentMean` (rank 1 = most negative/strongest). **Per-gene transfer loss:** `D_g = mean_o |R_og − R0_g| / (G−1)` (normalized mean absolute rank displacement, 0 = perfect rank transfer). Donor-equal weighting (organoids of same donor averaged first).

    15	
    16	## Decision rules (disjoint; two-sided bound)
    17	- `SIGNAL`: confirmatory 95% CI for partial ρ entirely **above +δ**.
    18	- `BOUND` (informative refutation): CI entirely **within (−δ, +δ)** — excludes decision-relevant association in *both* directions.
    19	- `INCONCLUSIVE`: otherwise. Reported as such; no reinterpretation.
    20	**δ candidate = 0.20, justification (decision-linked, Lock 1 ratifies or replaces with stated reasoning):** the endpoint exists to decide whether lability should *triage target trust* (deprioritize high-lability targets from 2D evidence). A rank association of 0.20 corresponds to lability ordering agreeing with displacement ordering only marginally better than chance for pairwise target comparisons (Kendall-equivalent ≈ 0.13, ~57% pairwise concordance) — below any threshold at which a triage rule would change shortlist composition in practice. δ is *not* derived from our exploratory point estimate (0.055) or from S3's different correlation type; Lock 1 must re-justify or amend it with the same decision argument before outcomes are touched.
